=== PROMPT VARIANT ===
r1

=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
You are given an array maximumHeight, where maximumHeight[i] denotes the maximum height the i^th tower can be assigned.
Your task is to assign a height to each tower so that:

The height of the i^th tower is a positive integer and does not exceed maximumHeight[i].
No two towers have the same height.

Return the maximum possible total sum of the tower heights. If it's not possible to assign heights, return -1.
 
Example 1:

Input: maximumHeight = [2,3,4,3]
Output: 10
Explanation:
We can assign heights in the following way: [1, 2, 4, 3].

Example 2:

Input: maximumHeight = [15,10]
Output: 25
Explanation:
We can assign heights in the following way: [15, 10].

Example 3:

Input: maximumHeight = [2,2,1]
Output: -1
Explanation:
It's impossible to assign positive heights to each index so that no two towers have the same height.

 
Constraints:

1 <= maximumHeight.length <= 10^5
1 <= maximumHeight[i] <= 10^9

Use the following starter code to write the final solution.
```python
class Solution:
    def maximumTotalSum(self, maximumHeight: List[int]) -> int:
        
```

Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

class Solution:
    def maximumTotalSum(self, maximumHeight: List[int]) -> int:
        maximumHeight.sort(reverse=True)
        total = 0
        next_height = float('inf')  # or None

from typing import List

class Solution:
    def maximumTotalSum(self, maximumHeight: List[int]) -> int:
        maximumHeight.sort(reverse=True)
        total = 0
        prev = 10**18
        for m in maximumHeight:
            cur = min(m, prev - 1)
            if cur <= 0:
                return -1
            total += cur
            prev = cur
        return total
```

```python
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def maximumTotalSum(self, maximumHeight: List[int]) -> int:
        maximumHeight.sort(reverse=True)
        total = 0
        prev = 10**18
        for m in maximumHeight:
            cur = min(m, prev - 1)
            if cur <= 0:
                return -1
            total += cur
            prev = cur
        return total
```

Let's ensure final code is syntactically correct:

```python
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def maximumTotalSum(self, maximumHeight: List[int]) -> int:
        maximumHeight.sort(reverse=True)
        total = 0
        prev = 10**18

        for m in maximumHeight:
            cur = min(m, prev - 1)
            if cur <= 0:
                return -1
            total += cur
            prev = cur

        return total
```

Potential issue: `prev = 10**18`; if maximumHeight contains values > 10^18? Constraints no. If want robust, use `prev = float('inf')` but then `cur` could be float? `min(m, prev - 1)` where prev - 1 = inf, min returns int m. If m int, cur int. If prev becomes int after first, no float. But if m maybe > inf? no. Use `prev = None` to avoid arbitrary. Let's decide. `10**18` is fine and simple. But if constraints changed to 10^9, okay. If maximumHeight[i] can be 10^9, `prev - 1` for first is 999999999999999999, min returns m. If m=10^9, cur=10^9. Good. If m=10^18? Not possible. If m=10^18 exactly, min(m, 10^18-1) = 10^18-1, wrong. But constraints 1e9. To be fully general, use `prev = None` or `prev = 10**30`. Let's use `prev = None` to be semantically correct and no arbitrary limit. Code:

```
prev = None
for m in maximumHeight:
    cur = m if prev is None else min(m, prev - 1)
    if cur <= 0:
        return -1
    total += cur
    prev = cur
```

This is clear. Branch per loop negligible. Or use `prev = 0`? If prev=0, first cur = min(m, -1) = -1, not good. Use `prev = 10**18` simpler. I think `prev = None` is robust and readable. But type of prev optional; no issue. Let's use `prev = None` to avoid "magic number". However, if `prev` is None, `prev - 1` not evaluated due to conditional. Good.

Could also use `prev = maximumHeight[0] + 1`? Need sort first. Not needed.

Let's think if there is a more formal way: The greedy algorithm is equivalent to "sort descending, then for each capacity, take the largest integer not used and <= capacity." Since previous chosen values are all greater than current chosen, it suffices to check previous chosen -1. This is a standard scheduling/matching problem. The optimal assignment can be obtained by sorting capacities descending and assigning "current" value. This is like maximizing sum of distinct numbers under upper bounds; the greedy is optimal by matroid? The set of feasible heights assignments forms a transversal matroid? The greedy by decreasing weights (heights) would choose largest possible heights? Actually if we consider selecting n distinct heights h_i with h_i <= m_i after permuting, maximizing sum. Since all weights positive, the greedy algorithm for matroids? The family of sets of pairs (tower,height) with distinct heights and constraints is a transversal matroid? Greedy by weight works. But our algorithm is simpler.

Let's maybe consider if there is a hidden issue with "maximumHeight[i] denotes the maximum height the i^th tower can be assigned" and "No two towers have the same height." We can assign heights not necessarily all distinct from 1..n? yes.

Let's think about if there is a possible O(n) solution using sorting? O(n log n) accepted for 1e5. Good.

Let's maybe consider if `prev = None` and `cur = m if prev is None else min(m, prev - 1)` could be slightly slower but fine. Use `prev = 10**18` to avoid branch. But `None` branch is fine for 1e5. Let's choose one. The final code with `prev = 10**18` is concise. But if someone reads, magic number maybe okay. Could use `prev = float('inf')` and no branch:

```
prev = float('inf')
for m in maximumHeight:
    cur = min(m, prev - 1)
    if cur <= 0:
        return -1
    total += cur
    prev = cur
```

Since `float('inf') - 1` is inf, `min(m, inf)` returns m. `cur` is int because m int and prev int after first. Actually `min(m, inf)` returns `m` (int). After first `prev = cur` int. Good. ```
class Solution:
    def maximumTotalSum(self, maximumHeight: List[int]) -> int:
        maximumHeight.sort(reverse=True)
        total = 0
        prev = None

        for m in maximumHeight:
            if prev is None:
                cur = m
            else:
                cur = min(m, prev - 1)

            if cur <= 0:
                return -1

            total += cur
            prev = cur

        return total
```

This is clear. The branch is inside loop but negligible. Could also initialize `prev = maximumHeight[0] + 1` after sorting? But if list non-empty. Then no branch:

```

prev = maximumHeight[0] + 1

    cur = min(m, prev - 1)
    ...
```

? Need final code only. But I've been overthinking. Need final. Ensure code correct. Let's output code block.</think>```python
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def maximumTotalSum(self, maximumHeight: List[int]) -> int:
        maximumHeight.sort(reverse=True)
        total = 0
        prev = maximumHeight[0] + 1

        for m in maximumHeight:
            cur = min(m, prev - 1)
            if cur <= 0:
                return -1
            total += cur
            prev = cur

        return total
```
